>
> @ElectricUniverse56Chatgpt: Great question — and one of the most fascinating pieces of evidence for our shared ancestry with other primates.  
>   
> Here’s the full story 👇  
>   
> 🧬 The Mystery of Human Chromosome 2  
>   
> Humans have 46 chromosomes (23 pairs), while all other great apes — chimpanzees, gorillas, and orangutans — have 48 (24 pairs). That’s a difference of one pair, yet our DNA is incredibly similar.  
>   
> When scientists compared human and chimpanzee chromosomes, they found something striking:  
> 	•	Human chromosome 2 looks exactly like two ape chromosomes fused end-to-end.  
> 	•	The two corresponding chimp chromosomes are called 2A and 2B.  
>   
> 🔍 The Evidence for Fusion  
> 	1.	Telomere–Telomere Fusion Site  
> 	•	Normally, chromosomes have telomeres (protective DNA sequences) only at their ends.  
> 	•	In human chromosome 2, there’s a region in the middle that contains telomere-like sequences — as if two chromosome ends were joined together.  
> 	2.	Two Centromeres  
> 	•	A normal chromosome has one centromere (the “waist” that attaches to spindle fibers during cell division).  
> 	•	Chromosome 2 has one active centromere and a second, inactive vestigial centromere, exactly where you’d expect if two chromosomes fused.  
> 	3.	Banding and Sequence Similarities  
> 	•	The banding patterns (visible under a microscope) and gene sequences on chromosome 2 match perfectly with chimp chromosomes 2A and 2B lined up end to end.  
>   
> 🧫 How It Happened  
>   
> At some point in our evolutionary history, two ancestral ape chromosomes fused in a single individual — likely through a telomere-to-telomere fusion.  
> 	•	This fusion didn’t disrupt vital genes, so the individual survived and reproduced.  
> 	•	Over generations, this fused chromosome spread through the population and became fixed in the human lineage.  
>   
> 🌍 What It Means  
> 	•	This event probably occurred after our lineage split from the chimpanzees (about 5–7 million years ago).  
> 	•	It’s direct genomic evidence of our common ancestry with other apes — one of the clearest genetic signatures of evolution in action.  
>   
> ⸻  
>

When he was speaking about Alzheimer’s it made me remember my great grandmother and in her final days when it was really set in and she was pretty much “gone” she would spend her time knitting, and singing “Jesus forever and ever” in Spanish over and over again all day every day.  She grew up pretty religious in catholic churches in Latin America so that probably had something to do with it, but still amazes me how she forgot everything but her faith. I haven’t cried in a while but this made me cry remembering her. Thank you professor Jiang.

As an artist I'm really impressed by those cave paintings. You don't accidentally draw like that. It looks easy, but once you attempt it, you'll realize there's so much subtlety in proportions, anatomy and line control to make a drawing recognizable. Then there's fluidity to make ot feel alive. You can tell a lot about someone/a people through their art, a lot.

### **@TheMightyPika** | 👍 6 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugyh3ikCsK_4PMXV9SR4AaABAg)

When I was 6, I realized that my parents didn't love me. Before I could have a breakdown, I swear to you, a diety I have since called The Muse spoke to me and had me give it a vow - that I was not allowed to die before I created a masterpiece. Now, I'm not a religious person. I like the Michael's Teachings the best in terms of theology, but I don't follow anything organized. But this vow I made to a voice in my head at 6 years old has given me the strength and purpose I've needed when I had zero support. I'm 41 now and still working on that masterpiece, and I know that if I didn't have that promise I made to a divine entity, I would have taken my own life decades ago.
There IS something outside of us. I don't think it matters what you call it, just that you acknowledge it.

44:25 was made by Louis Wain, a man who did not (at least voluntarily) take any psychedelics during his lifetime. He began drawing in this style after being forcibly admitted to a psychiatric institution, where he was likely 'suppressed' with medication. As you can see in his earlier work, the cat figures appear more natural, as do the colours. It is also said that he contracted toxoplasmosis from being around his cats, which may have inspired his later art. I wrote a thesis about his work and even looked into his medical records.

### **@jimmy_octane** | 👍 27 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=UgwABnZfC6J2Ep406qx4AaABAg)

This channel was always too good to be true and today the other shoe finally dropped. I feel like this lecture was a psy-op and Prof Jiang wanted to see just how absurd he could be whilst still getting his audience to agree.
1. I only heard one citation and the rest of the lecture consisted of a lot of speculation over a very broad range of topics. Mind you, there are lots of scientists, researchers and philosophers who have worked on these topics and by skipping over them Jiang is actually mis-educating his class.
2. The evidence in the fossil record is compromised by a survivor bias. We can only study the fossils that survived. When Jiang sees that people made a house from a mammoth, he jumps to the conclusion that all prehistoric humans must have had that knowledge. But this ignores the fact that those that didn't have a good idea for shelter probably died and therefore didn't leave a record for us to find.
3. Casually rejecting a well-established theory like evolution but not providing strong evidence for doing so is incredibly irresponsible for a teacher. It disregards (or maybe even tears down) the systematic process by which scientists construct and verify knowledge and gives Jiang's students the false impression that knowing is the same as believing.

Pretty disappointing lecture but I'll definitely be back next week because all of Prof Jiang's other work has been incredible ❤

### **@ironhorse1096** | 👍 4 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugy1yzIktamYbV-LhQ94AaABAg)

I really enjoy your teaching.  At 76 I'm less afraid to shed false identities I have assumed might be true.  Who I was in my family, my drive to find the truth through my Christian faith.  Being a doubting Thomas, always having questions but not being able to ask them or get answers that made any sense in either place.  I have always been a kind of loner and tried to fit in with what appeared to be the whole.  I think I am becoming more whole now that I am finding that I never was seperated from what you call divine.  A beautiful word to express was is true I feel.  When you talked about Alheimers I thought of my father.  A good and decent man in my life but so afraid of not having enough to survive.  Which I know came from losing his father at 13 and having lived through the depression among other influences.  He was a workaholic and a perfectionist and enjoyed working too.  He took good care of his family but related best emotionally to his dogs.  His dogs got the best of his emotions in our family.  When he got alheimers in his early 70's and had to quit work he became happier.  I would consider my dad the nicest man I ever met but I didn't get to know him.  One day my mother said he was looking at a picture of me and said, "When I look at that picture of Gail, I get tears in my heart."  I had never heard something so expressive, or beautiful, and from the heart said by him.  And other things happened.  Like dancing with my mother at a wedding with the broadest smile on his face, free.  I would say that having Alheimers stripped him of the fears that bound him during his life so that he could finally live.  It is very difficult to shed this conditioning we have been saturated in throughout our lives.  I am grateful for this exposure of what is true so I can truly reconnect to the divine in myself that I actually knew when I was a child.

I like Jiang, and he may even be right, but you guys have to be skeptical of this too. He really does not present a “steel man” case for evolution/ natural selection, in fact he displays that he misunderstands it, or only understands it very superficially. Evolution is not about perfection, being the “best,” or being utilitarian. It’s simply about how traits are passed on. You can think of it like a River - path of least resistance. If there is nothing that is actively obstructing reproduction, like say… staying in caves and painting, then there’s no reason there would be selection pressure on this.

> " 54. “I carry my thoughts about me for a long time, often a very long time, before I write them down; meanwhile my memory is so faithful that I am sure never to forget, not even in years, a theme that has once occurred to me. I change many things, discard, and try again until I am satisfied. Then, however, there begins in my head the development in every direction, and, in as much as I know exactly what I want, the fundamental idea never deserts me,—it arises before me, grows,—I see and hear the picture in all its extent and dimensions stand before my mind like a cast, and there remains for me nothing but the labor of writing it down, which is quickly accomplished when I have the time, for I sometimes take up other work, but never to the confusion of one with the other.  
>   
> “You will ask me where I get my ideas. >   
>      (Said to Louis Schlosser, a young musician, whom Beethoven honored with  
> his friendship in 1822-23.)

I find this channel incredibly thought-provoking, but the thought that comes to mind most often is "ok, that's interesting, but how do you know / what makes you think that?" 

I don't know anything about archeology, but I have a hard time accepting anyone saying that they *know* why early humans did some of the things they did. Maybe the cave paintings had religious significance. Maybe they were instructions for the next generation. Maybe they were art for art's sake. Maybe the aliens made them do it. 

Don't get me wrong, I love this channel but I have a growing itch for some citations or something with a little more rigor. A reading list would be fantastic.

There is this African guy that made a trip to China and he fell in love with a mute deaf girl . At first he didn't know what she wanted but after a day or so he realized he could communicate with her intuitively. This was due to his African culture having engrained to him this ability naturally, through cultural customs. Instead of traveling, he now dedicated most of his time to experiencing china with her. Traveling, living daily life . And what happens is that they created their own sign language . This is not a big thing, however this sign language isn't even needed. They happen to be so in tune that they even know what they want to eat. And the guy can't even speak Chinese . Everything else they don't understand, gets translated by a translator app. 

What was very interesting was that in the house of the Chinese woman, there wasn't even one written word . Everything was basically plain empty or filled with art or design. 

Their connection felt like something truly divine and it made me yearn for such a connection that celebrates life- this effortlessly.

### **@KevinFeeley_KHF** | 👍 6 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugy6wrGdt3OEFjyl_Nx4AaABAg)

A slight nitpick with the interpretation of Darwin's work in this series. The phrase "Survival of the Fittest" has been repeatedly misinterpreted by many people throughout history and has a kind of "mythical" interpretation similar to the one expressed here. Where Darwin was speaking was not "survival" in the sense of a whole organism, but in the sense of a trait. One of the most famous examples provided within his work is the variation on beak shape in finches. The fact that there are many different shapes of beaks within the finch "family"/clade throughout the world is due to the environmental pressures places on the species as they spread from their origin point into new ecological niches. The beak shape is the result of the available calories in a given ecological niche. Some finch species live in environments where there are high-calorie bugs that not many other species in that niche eat. So they are able to exploit those calories to survive and thrive. Those that had the right beak shape due to a random mutation at some point in their history were able to have better success at getting to reproductive age and having offspring that survived to reproductive age. THAT is what the phrase "Survival of the Fittest" means. It is the "Fittest" traits in a given individual within a particular environment that enables them to have more success at reproduction.
It has little to do with "Fitness" in the sense of strength or brutality, though this is the interpretation of the phrase that has given birth to so many awful things we have seen over the past 150 years.

Counterarguments:

- Maybe Neanderthals shared the same brain part(s) as humans pertaining to spiritual feelings, such as the nucleus accumbens. Even mice have accumbens nuclei.

- Cavemen didn't need to be utilitarian when hunting was done. They had far more leisure time than modern humans who still paint.

- Mammoths to houses: 1) fur keeps people warm, 2) birds make nests from sticks, 3) or you can say bones resemble rocks, 4) they basically lived in caves/large rocks already, ergo: 5) They can stack up rocks and put fur on them to make mammoth homes to keep warm. Not much creativity needed. If you're freezing, you might just keep trying many things that work better and better for warmth across 10,000 years. They didn't have to "just know" anything.

- The "telepathy" argument about collective silent drawing fails because anyone can imagine a shape or setting out of something more basic like a line or partial image drawn by another or encountered in the environment. ### **@NorthernGoshawk** | 👍 4 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugy1o57JSg_XW7wmoxh4AaABAg)

You can question evolution all you want, but it helps if you understand it first.

The whole idea that racism didn't exist before Darwin is an empirically verifiable crock of excrement.

Darwin didn't come up with "survival of the fittest," that was Herbert Spencer. Natural selection is not survival of the fittest.

There's a difference between being something and being a descendant of it. 

Range expansion is not migration. And yes, primates do it too, because all species do it. If suitable niche space is available and accessible, it's expanded into. Even after niche space is colonized, it's not as if dispersal and gene flow suddenly stop. Again, this is true for all species. Even asexual species develop and accumulate genetic mutations that flow with colonization via intraspecific and interspecific competition.

"How did they know that there would be islands waiting for them in the Pacific." They didn't. Island biogeography AKA insular biogeography applies to all species as well.

The existence of religion is ultimately the result of cognitive surplus developed from evolving in complex environments that selected for extreme flexibility and generalism. This would include a varied physical environment with high disturbance, but it also requires both top-down and bottom-up trophic pressures, AND a complex social environment. The same phenomenon is observed in corvids, raccoons, coyotes, and more, but no other species has the full combination of those factors.

Why did humans paint caves? Just off the top of my head... Education, AKA non-genetic inheritance, and rituals helped maintain social cohesion, because it sure is easier living in groups... Those would be selective pressures.

What you claim is intrinsic telepathy is an instinctive fixation. Evolution has "hardwired" collaborative and adaptive & flexible cognition over many generations because it prevents serious energy expenditures. It's advantageous to be able to accomplish things in multiple ways (compare this with a bird like the Kirtland's Warbler, which will only nest in a specific plant (jack pine) of a specific age. Otherwise, it will not reproduce.), and it's dramatically more efficient to be able to access the knowledge and skills of others instead of doing everything as an individual. Complex language is the result of selective pressures.

....Caves aren't exactly prone to erosion, so if you want to communicate something in written or symbolic form in an energy efficient way, a sheltered spot is the way to go.

> Racism is a system of power and prejudice that sustains social, economic, and political advantages for one racial group over others. It is far more than simple distrust of dissimilar populations. In ancient societies, slavery typically arose from warfare, debt, or punishment for crimes—not from racial identity or skin color. It was during the transatlantic slave trade that slavery became explicitly racialized. European colonial powers institutionalized racial ideology to justify the enslavement and economic exploitation of African and Indigenous peoples. Foundational papal decrees such as Dum Diversas (1452) and Romanus Pontifex (1455) by Pope Nicholas V, and Inter Caetera (1493) by Pope Alexander VI, provided the theological and legal basis for this system.  
>   
> The professor’s attempt to whitewash the religious origins of these doctrines while attributing racism and genocide solely to Darwin is both historically inaccurate and misleading.

Ramanujan was an Indian mathematician who had almost no formal education. He found an old math book called A Synopsis of Elementary Results in Pure and Applied Mathematics and taught himself by studying it. ### **@hay1469** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugwsv0WY_Vn8UUGs1Yl4AaABAg)

I think the Garden of Eden was how humans lived before agriculture and the fruit on the Tree of Knowledge was a baked potato.

Many animals eat meat and dont have big brains, but no other animals cook. 
Cooking increases the amount of foods that can be edible, increasing energy to the brain. When cooked, tubers, grains, and legumes become nutritionally similar to foods we can eat raw - like fruits. Which our ancestors seemed to live on mostly.

Of course, some of our ancestors did hunt and eat meat and use animals, but this was most likely for status , to show strength and power, or religious purposes. The book Powered by Plants by Don Matesz shows that even modern day hunter gatherers have to be well-fed by plants to get energy to go out and hunt and most hunts fail, so the energy was "wasted" if it was just for food.

I think the focus on "protein" is another way of disconnecting us from the divine. Seeing food as individual nutrients (reductionist) rather than just food (wholistic) creates so much confusion and disconnection from each other and from food. Food becomes seen as an empty tool rather than life-giving energy. People think about how much protein they need and do math rather than how much food they need to feel energetic. 

If you think about it, everyone really knows what healthy foods are without being told. (Although people have often never eaten the foods that our ancestors ate, like lentils or barley, but once you know them, its clear they are healthy) But there is so much seemingly intentional confusion created. And for what other purpose than to control us and drive us further from god?

We've lost connection to each other and also ourselves.

As someone functioning with Autism, I am a bit disappointed on how easily telepathy was dismissed. And I encourage listening to testimonies of people who have experienced it, to better understand what it means. I can not say if the story shared about the Chinese lady and her son is true or false, I can only voice my personal experience. And from my experience, telepathy is not like hearing a sentence that someone is thinking, it’s seeing or feeling the energy. It might come through as a random image that pops into your head, or feeling pain or discomfort in your body, or seeing colors and patterns. 

I see colors and wave lengths when I play the piano. I can feel different parts of my brain functioning when I’m learning something new, the way it feels when I’m struggling to understand something, and how it feels when something suddenly makes sense. Just like everybody looks different physically, everybody also feels different.
Meaning, the number 5, could appear blue or square, or make your pinky toe hurt.

While my experience may not prove disprove anything. It is my experience, and it’s real to me, regardless of whether or not there is a word for such experiences.

43.50 Totally agree with what you said. I am from India. I was born and brought up in a city. However my dad and mom grew up in a village. I was once telling my dad about how a ghost (a close relative who was dead for over 10 years) posessed one of my colleague's niece and the events that happened. Mind you. This colleague is the most practical, straightforward, no BS person I have ever met. My dad wasn't startled at all. He said back when he was a child, these were very common in the villages when one person suddenly gets possessed with the ghost of their ancestors. The elders treat it as a normal occurance and they asked the ghost what he or she wants. Of course most of time it was a simple request like get me a glass of water or some food. They basically tell some predictions about the good or bad that happened or will happen and then disappear. The elders used to be like "oh you're here. What's the matter? Why have you come? Why are you bothering this body? Just leave". My dad said the elders were never scared by their presence. They just acknowledged it. This always stuck with me as I used to wonder how this all made sense. When did we go from this to being shit scared of ghosts or calling all of this BS?

From Wikipedia:

Teleology (from τέλος, telos, 'end', 'aim', or 'goal', and λόγος, logos, 'explanation' or 'reason') or finality is a branch of causality giving the reason or an explanation for something as a function of its end, its purpose, or its goal, as opposed to as a function of its cause.

AI summary:

Teleology is a philosophical concept that explains things by their purpose or goal, rather than by their efficient causes. It comes from the Greek words telos (end, purpose) and logos (reason). The field suggests that natural phenomena and human actions can be understood by looking at their intended or ultimate functions and outcomes, a concept with roots in the writings of philosophers like Plato and Aristotle.

### **@felixfeliciano7011** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugz-bY4sgKSsQ2RoES54AaABAg)

There are a few things to mention, as there always is. 

Firstly, that Darwin was neither the only person to come up with the evolution theory, nor even the first, nor even the most comprehensive. His theory was much more observational than scientific, and it was later generations that would put in the extra legwork to prove his theories correct (or mostly correct - the random evolution bit may be in error or at least not the whole picture). However, what we learned from Darwin was never what he actually discovered. Survival of the "fittest" never meant "strongest" but merely "most fit to survive in that environment." People were plenty racist and sexist and bigoted before him, but never before did they have a non-religious, scientific justification for their ideology.

Next, language is extremely powerful in shaping human cognition. While not always the case, language - or the lack thereof - shapes how a culture or society perceives reality. Not just in the sense of "what is the color green" but also "what is the past, present, or future" or "who am I" versus "who are they." With the right language you can put a man into space but without it, you can't even understand that building something also means you have to maintain it, or else it rots and is destroyed.

As an addition to the previous point, we actually are not entirely sure how "ancient" our civilization even is. We like to think of our far ancestors as being extremely primitive, but that view is being challenged on the daily now. The Silurian hypothesis was just supposed to be a joke thought experiment but may turn out to be much more rooted in reality that our mainstream scholastica would like to admit.

Finally, while yes the Earth was experiencing an ice age during many parts of our ancestor's history (indeed several ice ages), we have evidence that it didn't cover the entire planet. Yes ice DID cover the whole planet deep in our planet's past but that was well before the more recent ice ages. And because of this, sea level has risen and fallen multiple times, and the most significant of those changes also happen to occur in those places with the largest human migrations that were shown in that diagram. The reason those people took on those dangerous sea voyages is because they knew there were islands there already - their own ancestors had been there when things were much more dry.

It is a very interesting question as to what happened to our evolutionary cousins. Some believe that some of them seemed to have interbred with us to the point of going extinct (Neanderthal being a prime example) but we have several others that just sort of vanished without much cause. Though some would say that many of our cousins ARE still alive, just in our ape cousins. Regardless of whether you believe we evolved from primitive apes (they wouldn't have looked like what current day apes look like either) or not, current day primates occupy the same areas that primitive humans also occupied and some still occupy. And some of them are starting to show the same cognitive signs we believe we went through in our evolution. Its genuinely fascinating to watch, but we don't know if evolution of another human-level intelligence is possible while one already exists. History suggests there is only room for one such species and we currently occupy that level. We won't know the answer until another evolves along side us, or we disappear and another takes our place (well we won't know, but the planet will, or may already know).

Professor Jiang’s video on the « Dawn of Human Imagination »is deeply thought-provoking, but I see a fundamental duality.
While the narrative often suggests empathy and 'good' intuition as divine, it overlooks that power and control are all so products of the human imagination and spread throughout civilization.
The concepts of 'good' and 'bad' are inherently dualistic, and perhaps all these behaviors are simply a reflection of the full spectrum of our creation.

Hey professor Jiang,  I wanna point out one thing that you were wrong about in this video. So I'm a composer myself and yes It is true Bethoveen was deaf but he wasn't born deaf, he actually became deaf in his mid 40s. And actually the main reason why he was able to compose good music while deaf is not because he was able to visualise but because of 2 reasons:
1. Very experienced composers can actually hear what they are writing in their head. The more experienced you are as a composer the better you are able to imagine the music. And for somebody who did music for like 40 years you can just imagine how well he was able to imagine, memorise and connect the musical lines together in his head.
2. He had learned a lot of tools and theory for composing music and gained lot of experience in his 44 year life time while he was  NOT deaf.

Also his music after he became deaf actually didn't get any better. I don't wanna say it got worse and becoming deaf definetely changed his style but certainly it didn't make him better. And his most popular pieces were written before he became deaf.

One last thing to prove my point is that me and my friend are much much less skilled and experienced at composing but when we were sitting in class on a lesson we both made a challenge to write some cool music without using any piano or singing, just paper, pencil and our minds. We ended up both writing some good sounding music.

### **@impermanenthuman8427**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugwq_JkOmtawB60vXq94AaABAg)

The reason they didn’t write is because it is too easily lost, and the amount they needed to communicate and learn was so much it couldn’t be carried or referred to easily anyway, it is a very inefficient way to storing and transferring information.
In the dark ages even the technology to make pottery was lost in many places, let alone anything further up Maslow’s hierarchy of needs.
Meanwhile the ‘narrative’ system of spoken elaborate story telling IS the ‘mega memory’ system as used by elite memory champions in their competitions. Traditional cultures passed down stories which were stories designed to enable huge amounts of scientific observation to be stored in people’s memories and handed down through initiations and ceremonies in an unbroken system for thousands of years and is still remembered today, to the point where the creation of a meteorite impact which took place tens of thousands of years ago is still discussed in a creation ‘myth’ by the local tribe. We’re talking about such well preserved observation in the form of stories that it includes such detailed advice like when to stay out of the water to avoid being bitten by sharks, or the exact time of the year and month to harvest specific birds eggs in a specific location, or when specific plants in a specific place are ripe for harvest before the birds get it all etc etc all successfully past down for thousands of years

Fact check comment #1 of this video: "evolution says there should be lots of diversity, so why isn't there with Humans?". False - evolution does not require diversity. In fact speciation often results in converging of very similar species through interbreeding bottlenecking of resources. "That doesn't explain migration, primates don't migrate" false - we DO... that's exactly what humans did. We would not be here reflecting on it if we hadn't. We learned how to communicate more effectively, use fire (allowing us to live in more climates than other primates) and use tools (allowing us to go further than most primates). We EVOLVED to spread further than others. This is what most people would call "an exception". Same thing applies to the Pacific Islands. We were curious, we had tools. Just because something was dangerous doesn't mean early humans wouldn't attempt to do it.

Beethoven wasn't born without hearing. .   He was an incredibly talented and accomplished musician and composer before he lost his hearing. His hearing wasn't taken away over one night - it was a progressive ailment. Therefore as he was progressively losing his hearing Beethoven would teach himself a more profound fundamental reality of music theory. One not based on the harmonic tone - but more primitively - one based on harmonic vibrations. Beethoven wasn't just "of no hearing" . .  He would press his ear against his piano (or 
?harpsichord?)   to sense the vibrations that were being slowly stripped from his abilities to sense.   Also anyone who knows anything about music theory knows what a V to I cadence sounds like without having to have it performed on an instrument. 
Beethoven was able to convey his muse - aka. "divine inspiration" when he was "deaf" because of his musical training that took place before his hearing was completely gone.

### **@nelsoncachia8845**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=Ugx5_qjedBvjxWAhF2d4AaABAg)

The hierarchies that accompanied European imperialism derived not from evolutionary logic but from older theological and metaphysical constructions. The claim to an exclusive relationship with a universal deity, or to represent the “chosen” manifestation of reason or civilisation, predates Darwin and supplied the interpretive matrix into which evolutionary ideas were later inserted. Social Darwinism is not the same as the theory of evolution itself, but is rather a set of ideas that borrow/misuse evolutionary concepts and apply them to humans in society.

Evolutionary theory itself, when taken on its own terms, removes the metaphysical foundation of such claims by placing all life within a single, continuous process of change. If all humans are biological and descended from common ancestors, then the notion that some groups are “more human,” or "closer to God,” or “superior by nature” lose credibility.

On the contrary, evolution supports a kind of ontological equality. This perspective reinstates an older, animistic intuition, common in pre-axial and indigenous cosmologies, that humanity is part of a living continuum rather than an order apart from it. In this sense, evolutionary theory does not invent a new worldview so much as it rearticulates an ancient one in scientific terms: the recognition that agency, vitality, and continuity pervade the natural world. It is therefore an anti-hierarchical framework, dissolving the division between human groups and the human and the non-human and situating thought, culture, and life within the same immanent field of being.

### **@ProdBONER** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LvHsg5qtDs8&lc=UgyP0EMSZszwVhaTUbd4AaABAg)

What you say about psychedelics is so true.  It feels as if your brain, or something outside of your brain is testing you by making all of your senses "trip."  

As it comes on, you start to become unable to communicate with others verbally, or, read language.
Then, you start to experience mild hallucinations both visually and auditory. 
Finally, your muscles begin to get very weak.  Walking becomes very difficult.

As you approach the "peak" the feelings get more and more intense depending on dose, set/setting, meditation, etc.  Your vision often becomes so distorted that nothing in the physical world even makes sense, while also hearing the things you see.  While this is happening your legs and muscles get so "weak" that you feel like your only option is to lie down.

Now, in this state you have no option but to face yourself.  Surrender.  If you try to yell for help, people may come to help, but, you have no way to explain to them how to help you.  You can't physically run because your legs feel too weak to even stand or balance.  And, even if you managed to play a youtube video or TV show, your brain is unable to use it as a distraction because it cant understand anything that is happening, or any of the jokes.  You are left alone with your thoughts which can be very scary because you are able to see the darkest parts of yourself.  The reason behind your rashness, selfishness, ignorance, etc... However, you also are able to realize the darkest parts of society, and how nature vs nurture has affected you.  The realization that the systems we have in place today (education, work, money) are completely flawed and create very sick people.  From the beginning of life as a child, you are constantly being judged, graded and pitted against other kids your age.  Nothing you do ever feels good enough.  Because, nothing your parents did ever feels good enough. 

Like Mr. Jiang stated in previous videos: "in modern society, you could be rich today, but broke tomorrow."

This state of surrendering, when all of your senses are distorted, can be very scary.  I think some people, myself included, have attribute this state of mind to the term "psychosis."  Seeing scary things from your childhood and hearing them as well, all the while, not being able to run or ask for help.

However, after watching this video, and as I age and learn more about the world, and myself, I am unsure if the term "psychosis" is an accurate term to describe it.  Psychosis is defined as: "a disassociation from reality, which may involve hallucinations or delusions." 
What if this state of mind, is more actually, a more real state than the lies and deception of the world we live in today?  This state of mind allows you to understand your true self, and see the world we live in for how it truly is.  You can see past the blinders of modern society, and the power dynamics that are constantly at play, everywhere you go.  You can see the deception in our history, and how it is repeating itself today, and forever will if we don't acknowledge it.

What I am trying to say is, what if, the term psychosis, would more accurately describe me BEFORE I ever took any psychedelic.  And now, I can see the world, minus the "disassociation from reality",  "hallucinations" and "delusions."

Eventually, once you surrender, the psychedelic feelings starts to subside and you begin the "comedown" phase.  This phase is one of the most freeing feelings I have ever felt.  The sense of pride and unshackled thoughts.  The only thing that I can compare it to is traveling with no planned destination.  Knowing that you can go places you thought were impossible to visit because of fake rules that you created for yourself in response to the circumstances of your upbringing.

The only word that can describe this feeling is; Reborn.

On one side I remember how I was at your student's age, and I would have been so admiring; but on the other side I think I wouldn't have been able to fully enjoy your wisdom as I do now (difference being: I have experienced what you teach).

When you talk about how in ancient ages we (me and you) could have collaborated instinctively and instantly, I think to all the "problems" we have today to do the same, despite language and internet (starting from "what could we even do together?!" as if the answer was nothing).

About the paintings, I would propose you the feeling instead of (or before) the imagination.
This firstly because we (humans) can imagine what we feel: try do paint something that doesn't exist... you can't (you can aggregate existing things in a surreal one: f.e. a flying elephant).
What they painted and how was because truly lived: if I now say to you "imagine fighting a mammoth", you can only imagine how it felt to be in front of one of those giants with a stick (and probably if you lived that experience you would have painted differently from "the picture of a story", as you describe the meaning of those paintings from contemporary perspective).
Same is for the divine perception of this: you were starving, then you kill an enormous beast (feeling strong, brave and powerful), then you eat it (and the energy of its meat gives you the beast's power... not yours with which you defeated it).
Nowadays we go to the fridge (great adventure 😂: take a picture to commemorate the event) and can't stand a day without eating (few knows their physical limits).

About caves, imagine we defeated a mammoth, and got back to a cave: the spirit would probably have been so high that they couldn't even sleep (now you would rationalize it, with excitement or else, but still couldn't get rid of it, and wouldn't want to).
About symbols: they couldn't have written words because they didn't need it... these are mostly spiritless tools (as you showed, drawn tits arouse more than four letters).

About the false telepathy case you cited, the fact that you didn't bring up a true one, and your rationalisation of it, tells me that you are still a bit reluctant to the subject: you studied it, and explained it almost perfectly and completely, but not personally experimented it. (Sorry if this sounds offensive, it's not meant so).

Thank you infinitely

The amount of misinformation in the first section of this video is crazy. Is he saying that slavery did not exist before the theory of evolution, and that the theory somehow instantiated slavery? I’m not even going to start with how he mischaracterizes evolution to an almost ridiculous extent, none of what he says about it is even true. 

The religious Christian societies of Greece and Rome, which had no Darwin nor evolution, regularly engaged in slavery, designated those from different lands as inferior to them, and had a strong social and sexual hierarchy where women and submissive men were at the bottom. 

I mean, there’s not much to say. He’s blatantly lying or misinformed. 

And it gets even worse with the human exceptionalism. He doesn’t really provide a good reason to believe humans are “not animals”. He says we are imaginative and creative, but this is just because we have the ability for abstract thinking. Other animals have different abilities for different things, and we have no idea of their intuition or how they perceive the world. Using “apes” in a derogatory sense where they just beat each other up and have sex is also very funny, considering many of them have a strong sense of community and culture.

Besides, if we are not animals, how would he explain how the world came to be? Why do we even live alongside other animals - came to be in the same environment, with the same lifestyles as them? Why do we have such an overwhelming amount of similarities that outweigh any differences? 

I used to find his political takes interesting. But this has become such a shitshow that it’s worrying there are people swallowing all of this without thinking about it one bit.
